Like, I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then somehow it’s not done, and then we’re dealing with the fallout. So yes, it does interfere with keeping a normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I think it’s probably a combination, though the part that’s really been pushing me to make this appointment is the follow-through piece.

Like after audits or mentorship events, I’ll start the follow-up items with every intention of finishing them. I’ll open the email, start the review note, or pull up the form, and then somehow days later I find out the final email never got sent, or the note wasn’t completed, or the form is still sitting there unfinished. And then I’m sort of scrambling and thinking, how did I not finish that?

So… I don’t know if that answers it exactly. It feels less like I’m choosing not to do things and more like I start them and lose the thread before they’re actually done.

Because detail is supposed to be my strength. That’s kind of my whole job. So yes, it happens beyond just forgetting a follow-up task, but I’m sorry, I realize I’m still talking mostly about work examples. That’s just where it shows up and embarrasses me the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble paying attention to details in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… yes, but it’s harder for me to give clean examples because I don’t have, like, a reviewed workpaper proving it afterward. At home it’ll be more like I think I read something carefully and then realize I missed a detail—though honestly, the examples that really stick in my mind are still the work ones.\n\nLike with reports, I know I should not be rushing, and I’ll still miss that a date changed, or I’ll transpose a number, or I’ll leave some dumb placeholder note in there like “confirm this,” and then someone else sees it. It’s embarrassing because details are supposed to be the thing I’m good at.\n\nDoctor: Are these mistakes and oversights affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with friends it’s less obvious maybe, but at home, yes. My husband has started taking over things like bills or commitments I said I would handle, because I’ll agree to do them and then somehow it just… doesn’t get completed reliably. And then he’s stuck either reminding me again or just doing it himself, which is not great for a marriage.\n\nAnd at work—sorry, I know you asked family or friends, but that’s where I feel the panic the most—it’s gotten to where a client review was actually affected by something I missed or didn’t finish. I’m also worried people are starting to question whether I can really handle the promotion I got. That sounds dramatic, but it’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wouldn’t have described it that way before, maybe, but yes. Like if I’m sitting through a long review, it starts to feel physically uncomfortable, like there’s this pressure building up inside me.\n\nI’ll try to sit still and look normal, but then I need to stand up or walk to the printer or suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll invent some reason to move. And sometimes it’s not even that I need anything from the printer, it’s just… I can’t keep sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with follow-through and attention to detail are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just work stress, or that everyone drops the ball sometimes, but… it’s more than that now.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. And since I just got promoted, I keep thinking people are looking at me like, “Can she actually handle this?” Which is a terrible feeling, because I worked so hard to get there.\n\nAnd then at home it spills over too. My husband has started taking over bills and commitments that I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I hate saying that out loud. It makes me feel like I’m creating extra work for him, and it definitely affects how I feel day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not like big reckless things, but in conversations I definitely do it.\n\nI’ll jump in before someone finishes, especially with junior auditors, because I can see where they’re going—or I think I can—and the answer feels really urgent in my head. Like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it or the moment will pass. And then I’ve already blurted it out.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I don’t mean to be rude or dismissive. Sometimes I’ll realize halfway through that they weren’t even finished, and then I’m trying to backpedal and say, “Sorry, go ahead,” but I’ve already interrupted them.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity when you're not at work or under stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not only work stress. I mean, work is where it’s most obvious because there are reviews and clients and, you know, everyone can see it. But it shows up at home too.\n\nLike, I’ll say I’m going to handle a bill or some commitment, and then it just… doesn’t get finished reliably. My husband has started taking over some of those things because he can’t really count on me to follow through, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at work, it’s gotten serious enough that mistakes and unfinished follow-up affected a client review, so now I’m worried people are wondering if I can actually handle the promotion. So, yes, even when I try to tell myself it’s just stress, it’s affecting more than just work.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in quiet or low-stress situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. That’s part of what makes it hard to explain, because it’s not only when things are hectic.\n\nLike, if I’m reading at home—something I want to do, not work—I’ll still end up checking something on my phone, or getting up, or humming without really noticing. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just kind of waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and I’ll start talking or looking for something to do.\n\nIt’s like pure quiet is uncomfortable somehow. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was a kid, honestly. I don’t think it just started in adulthood.\n\nMy mom actually still jokes about this—probably not in a mean way, but you know, family jokes stick with you—that I could ace a test and then forget the project that was literally sitting in my backpack. And my elementary school report cards were kind of the same theme: “bright,” but lost assignments, rushed through little details, talked out of turn, needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to think that was just me being scattered or maybe bored, because I did well enough on tests. But looking back, yes, those attention and impulsive things were already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety in situations where you're expected to be quiet, like in a meeting or at home watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Yes. Long meetings are probably the clearest example.\n\nIf I’m sitting in a long review, especially one where I’m supposed to just listen quietly, it starts to feel almost physical—like this pressure building up inside me. I’ll try to sit still and look normal, but after a while I feel like I need to stand up or move somehow. Sometimes I’ll invent a reason to walk to the printer or get water or check something, even if it’s not really necessary.\n\nAt home it can happen too, like with a movie, though meetings are worse because I’m aware I’m supposed to be composed. It’s not just boredom exactly—it feels uncomfortable to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to any significant problems in your personal or professional relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, unfortunately yes.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten past the “oh, she’s just busy” kind of thing. I’ve made mistakes, and the bigger issue is probably the follow-up—like I’ll mean to circle back on something, and then it’s just… not done. One client review was affected by that, which was really embarrassing. And I’ve been up for more responsibility, or at least that’s been discussed, but now I worry people are wondering if I can actually handle it.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband is pretty patient, but he’s taken over bills and things I said I would handle. Commitments I agreed to, appointments, that kind of thing. Not because he wants to control it, but because he doesn’t really trust that I’ll finish it reliably. Which is hard to say out loud, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience restlessness or fidgetiness when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the way it showed up then was maybe less like me saying “I feel restless” and more that I just couldn’t quite stay on track in the classroom.\n\nMy report cards in elementary school were always this weird mix of “bright” and then all these comments about losing assignments, rushing past the small details, talking out of turn, needing reminders to finish my work. My mom still brings up that I could do really well on a test and then completely forget the project that was sitting in my backpack the whole time.\n\nSo… yes, I think there was that kind of fidgety, unsettled thing there, but it got written up more as me being chatty or careless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention and impulsivity interfering with your daily routines or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, work is the obvious place where it shows up, but outside of work it’s definitely affecting normal day-to-day life too.\n\nAt home, my husband has kind of… quietly taken over certain things, like bills and commitments I said I’d handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. Which sounds terrible when I say it that way. It’s not that I don’t care—it’s more like I’ll agree to something, and then it slips, or I don’t follow through the way I meant to.\n\nAnd then work bleeds into it too, because when I make mistakes or don’t follow up, I carry that stress home. There was a client review that was affected, and now I worry people are questioning whether I can handle a promotion. So even when I’m home, I’m thinking about what I forgot, or what I need to fix, and then the home things pile up too. It’s all sort of tangled together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention and impulsivity significantly interfering with your ability to complete daily tasks and maintain a regular routine?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I wish it was just a matter of being a little disorganized, but it’s more than that.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow up on have gotten noticeable. There was a client review that was actually affected because I didn’t finish or circle back the way I should have, and now I keep thinking, okay, if they were considering me for a promotion, are they now wondering if I can really handle it?\n\nAnd then at home it’s the same kind of thing, just with different consequences. My husband has had to take over bills and commitments I’d already agreed to handle because, honestly, he can’t really count on me to complete them reliably. Which is embarrassing. Like, I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then somehow it’s not done, and then we’re dealing with the fallout. So yes, it does interfere with keeping a normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t, like… I don’t have a neat history to give you where I can say, “Yes, at this age, this doctor said this, and I took this medication.” It’s more that people have commented on different things over the years, or I’ve wondered about it myself, especially when stuff starts piling up.\n\nSorry, I’m probably making that less direct than you asked. I guess the short answer is: I’m not sure I’ve ever had a formal diagnosis or treatment for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity are more related to inattention, hyperactivity, or a combination of both?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably a combination, though the part that’s really been pushing me to make this appointment is the follow-through piece.\n\nLike after audits or mentorship events, I’ll start the follow-up items with every intention of finishing them. I’ll open the email, start the review note, or pull up the form, and then somehow days later I find out the final email never got sent, or the note wasn’t completed, or the form is still sitting there unfinished. And then I’m sort of scrambling and thinking, how did I not finish that?\n\nSo… I don’t know if that answers it exactly. It feels less like I’m choosing not to do things and more like I start them and lose the thread before they’re actually done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation). Sometimes I’ll realize halfway through that they weren’t even finished, and then I’m trying to backpedal and say, “Sorry, go ahead,” but I’ve already interrupted them.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity when you're not at work or under stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not only work stress. So, yes, even when I try to tell myself it’s just stress, it’s affecting more than just work.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in quiet or low-stress situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. That’s part of what makes it hard to explain, because it’s not only when things are hectic.\n\nLike, if I’m reading at home—something I want to do, not work—I’ll still end up checking something on my phone, or getting up, or humming without really noticing. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just kind of waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and I’ll start talking or looking for something to do.\n\nIt’s like pure quiet is uncomfortable somehow. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was a kid, honestly. I don’t think it just started in adulthood.\n\nMy mom actually still jokes about this—probably not in a mean way, but you know, family jokes stick with you—that I could ace a test and then forget the project that was literally sitting in my backpack. One client review was affected by that, which was really embarrassing. Commitments I agreed to, appointments, that kind of thing. Not because he wants to control it, but because he doesn’t really trust that I’ll finish it reliably. Yes, I think so. I mean, I do fidget, but that’s not what’s getting me in trouble.

The attention and follow-through stuff is affecting real things now.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is… I keep dropping the follow-through on things. Like after audits, or even mentorship events at work, I’ll start the follow-up items with every intention of finishing them, and then a few days later I realize the final email never went out, or the review note is still sitting there half-done, or some form wasn’t actually completed.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because I’m an accountant, so you’d think I’d be very on top of that kind of thing. I mean, I am in a lot of ways, but then something small but important just doesn’t get finished, and I only find out after the fact. That’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I probably need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details in other areas of your life, not just with work tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the details matter so much, and I know better than to rush those things. I’ll look at a report later and realize I missed that a date had changed, or I transposed a number, or—this is the one that makes me want to crawl under my desk—I left some placeholder comment sitting in the workpaper like “confirm this” or something.\n\nAnd then I think, how did I not see that? Because detail is supposed to be my strength. That’s kind of my whole job. So yes, it happens beyond just forgetting a follow-up task, but I’m sorry, I realize I’m still talking mostly about work examples. That’s just where it shows up and embarrasses me the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble paying attention to details in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… yes, but it’s harder for me to give clean examples because I don’t have, like, a reviewed workpaper proving it afterward. At home it’ll be more like I think I read something carefully and then realize I missed a detail—though honestly, the examples that really stick in my mind are still the work ones.\n\nLike with reports, I know I should not be rushing, and I’ll still miss that a date changed, or I’ll transpose a number, or I’ll leave some dumb placeholder note in there like “confirm this,” and then someone else sees it. It’s embarrassing because details are supposed to be the thing I’m good at.\n\nDoctor: Are these mistakes and oversights affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with friends it’s less obvious maybe, but at home, yes. My husband has started taking over things like bills or commitments I said I would handle, because I’ll agree to do them and then somehow it just… doesn’t get completed reliably. And then he’s stuck either reminding me again or just doing it himself, which is not great for a marriage.\n\nAnd at work—sorry, I know you asked family or friends, but that’s where I feel the panic the most—it’s gotten to where a client review was actually affected by something I missed or didn’t finish. I’m also worried people are starting to question whether I can really handle the promotion I got. That sounds dramatic, but it’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wouldn’t have described it that way before, maybe, but yes. Like if I’m sitting through a long review, it starts to feel physically uncomfortable, like there’s this pressure building up inside me.\n\nI’ll try to sit still and look normal, but then I need to stand up or walk to the printer or suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll invent some reason to move. And sometimes it’s not even that I need anything from the printer, it’s just… I can’t keep sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with follow-through and attention to detail are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just work stress, or that everyone drops the ball sometimes, but… it’s more than that now.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. And since I just got promoted, I keep thinking people are looking at me like, “Can she actually handle this?” Which is a terrible feeling, because I worked so hard to get there.\n\nAnd then at home it spills over too. My husband has started taking over bills and commitments that I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I hate saying that out loud. It makes me feel like I’m creating extra work for him, and it definitely affects how I feel day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not like big reckless things, but in conversations I definitely do it.\n\nI’ll jump in before someone finishes, especially with junior auditors, because I can see where they’re going—or I think I can—and the answer feels really urgent in my head. Like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it or the moment will pass. And then I’ve already blurted it out.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I don’t mean to be rude or dismissive. Sometimes I’ll realize halfway through that they weren’t even finished, and then I’m trying to backpedal and say, “Sorry, go ahead,” but I’ve already interrupted them.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity when you're not at work or under stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not only work stress. I mean, work is where it’s most obvious because there are reviews and clients and, you know, everyone can see it. But it shows up at home too.\n\nLike, I’ll say I’m going to handle a bill or some commitment, and then it just… doesn’t get finished reliably. My husband has started taking over some of those things because he can’t really count on me to follow through, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at work, it’s gotten serious enough that mistakes and unfinished follow-up affected a client review, so now I’m worried people are wondering if I can actually handle the promotion. So, yes, even when I try to tell myself it’s just stress, it’s affecting more than just work.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in quiet or low-stress situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. That’s part of what makes it hard to explain, because it’s not only when things are hectic.\n\nLike, if I’m reading at home—something I want to do, not work—I’ll still end up checking something on my phone, or getting up, or humming without really noticing. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just kind of waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and I’ll start talking or looking for something to do.\n\nIt’s like pure quiet is uncomfortable somehow. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was a kid, honestly. I don’t think it just started in adulthood.\n\nMy mom actually still jokes about this—probably not in a mean way, but you know, family jokes stick with you—that I could ace a test and then forget the project that was literally sitting in my backpack. And my elementary school report cards were kind of the same theme: “bright,” but lost assignments, rushed through little details, talked out of turn, needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to think that was just me being scattered or maybe bored, because I did well enough on tests. But looking back, yes, those attention and impulsive things were already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety in situations where you're expected to be quiet, like in a meeting or at home watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Yes. Long meetings are probably the clearest example.\n\nIf I’m sitting in a long review, especially one where I’m supposed to just listen quietly, it starts to feel almost physical—like this pressure building up inside me. I’ll try to sit still and look normal, but after a while I feel like I need to stand up or move somehow. Sometimes I’ll invent a reason to walk to the printer or get water or check something, even if it’s not really necessary.\n\nAt home it can happen too, like with a movie, though meetings are worse because I’m aware I’m supposed to be composed. It’s not just boredom exactly—it feels uncomfortable to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to any significant problems in your personal or professional relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, unfortunately yes.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten past the “oh, she’s just busy” kind of thing. I’ve made mistakes, and the bigger issue is probably the follow-up—like I’ll mean to circle back on something, and then it’s just… not done. One client review was affected by that, which was really embarrassing. And I’ve been up for more responsibility, or at least that’s been discussed, but now I worry people are wondering if I can actually handle it.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband is pretty patient, but he’s taken over bills and things I said I would handle. Commitments I agreed to, appointments, that kind of thing. Not because he wants to control it, but because he doesn’t really trust that I’ll finish it reliably. Which is hard to say out loud, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience restlessness or fidgetiness when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the way it showed up then was maybe less like me saying “I feel restless” and more that I just couldn’t quite stay on track in the classroom.\n\nMy report cards in elementary school were always this weird mix of “bright” and then all these comments about losing assignments, rushing past the small details, talking out of turn, needing reminders to finish my work. My mom still brings up that I could do really well on a test and then completely forget the project that was sitting in my backpack the whole time.\n\nSo… yes, I think there was that kind of fidgety, unsettled thing there, but it got written up more as me being chatty or careless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention and impulsivity interfering with your daily routines or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, work is the obvious place where it shows up, but outside of work it’s definitely affecting normal day-to-day life too.\n\nAt home, my husband has kind of… quietly taken over certain things, like bills and commitments I said I’d handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. Which sounds terrible when I say it that way. It’s not that I don’t care—it’s more like I’ll agree to something, and then it slips, or I don’t follow through the way I meant to.\n\nAnd then work bleeds into it too, because when I make mistakes or don’t follow up, I carry that stress home. There was a client review that was affected, and now I worry people are questioning whether I can handle a promotion. So even when I’m home, I’m thinking about what I forgot, or what I need to fix, and then the home things pile up too. It’s all sort of tangled together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention and impulsivity significantly interfering with your ability to complete daily tasks and maintain a regular routine?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I wish it was just a matter of being a little disorganized, but it’s more than that.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow up on have gotten noticeable. There was a client review that was actually affected because I didn’t finish or circle back the way I should have, and now I keep thinking, okay, if they were considering me for a promotion, are they now wondering if I can really handle it?\n\nAnd then at home it’s the same kind of thing, just with different consequences. My husband has had to take over bills and commitments I’d already agreed to handle because, honestly, he can’t really count on me to complete them reliably. Which is embarrassing. Like, I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then somehow it’s not done, and then we’re dealing with the fallout. So yes, it does interfere with keeping a normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t, like… I don’t have a neat history to give you where I can say, “Yes, at this age, this doctor said this, and I took this medication.” It’s more that people have commented on different things over the years, or I’ve wondered about it myself, especially when stuff starts piling up.\n\nSorry, I’m probably making that less direct than you asked. I guess the short answer is: I’m not sure I’ve ever had a formal diagnosis or treatment for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity are more related to inattention, hyperactivity, or a combination of both?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably a combination, though the part that’s really been pushing me to make this appointment is the follow-through piece.\n\nLike after audits or mentorship events, I’ll start the follow-up items with every intention of finishing them. I’ll open the email, start the review note, or pull up the form, and then somehow days later I find out the final email never got sent, or the note wasn’t completed, or the form is still sitting there unfinished. And then I’m sort of scrambling and thinking, how did I not finish that?\n\nSo… I don’t know if that answers it exactly. It feels less like I’m choosing not to do things and more like I start them and lose the thread before they’re actually done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more because you have trouble sitting still and being patient, or because you have trouble remembering details and following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the remembering details and following through, honestly. I mean, I can get impatient sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is that I’ll start these follow-up items after audits or mentorship events, and I fully mean to finish them.\n\nLike I’ll draft the final email, or start the review note, or pull up whatever form needs to be completed—and then days later I find out it never actually got sent or finished. And then I’m backtracking and feeling awful because in my mind I had already “done” it, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of circling around it, but yes—the follow-through part is the bigger issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity are causing more problems in your life than your restlessness and fidgetiness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I do fidget, but that’s not what’s getting me in trouble.\n\nThe attention and follow-through stuff is affecting real things now. At work, my mistakes and unfinished follow-up have gotten to the point where a client review was affected, and I keep worrying people are looking at me like, can she actually handle this promotion? Which is embarrassing, because I’ve worked really hard to get here.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I said I would handle because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. Not because I don’t care, but because I’ll agree to something and then it just doesn’t get completed. So yes, that part is causing more problems than just restlessness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yes, I think it is across settings. I mean, work is where it’s the most obvious because there are consequences—my mistakes and unfinished follow-up have gotten bad enough that a client review was affected, and now I’m worried people are questioning whether I can really handle the promotion.

And then he’s stuck either reminding me again or just doing it himself, which is not great for a marriage.\n\nAnd at work—sorry, I know you asked family or friends, but that’s where I feel the panic the most—it’s gotten to where a client review was actually affected by something I missed or didn’t finish. I’m also worried people are starting to question whether I can really handle the promotion I got. That sounds dramatic, but it’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wouldn’t have described it that way before, maybe, but yes. Like if I’m sitting through a long review, it starts to feel physically uncomfortable, like there’s this pressure building up inside me.\n\nI’ll try to sit still and look normal, but then I need to stand up or walk to the printer or suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll invent some reason to move. And sometimes it’s not even that I need anything from the printer, it’s just… I can’t keep sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with follow-through and attention to detail are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just work stress, or that everyone drops the ball sometimes, but… it’s more than that now.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow through on have gotten to the point where a client review was affected. And since I just got promoted, I keep thinking people are looking at me like, “Can she actually handle this?” Which is a terrible feeling, because I worked so hard to get there.\n\nAnd then at home it spills over too. My husband has started taking over bills and commitments that I said I would handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I hate saying that out loud. It makes me feel like I’m creating extra work for him, and it definitely affects how I feel day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not like big reckless things, but in conversations I definitely do it.\n\nI’ll jump in before someone finishes, especially with junior auditors, because I can see where they’re going—or I think I can—and the answer feels really urgent in my head. Sometimes I’ll realize halfway through that they weren’t even finished, and then I’m trying to backpedal and say, “Sorry, go ahead,” but I’ve already interrupted them.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity when you're not at work or under stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not only work stress. I mean, work is where it’s most obvious because there are reviews and clients and, you know, everyone can see it. But it shows up at home too.\n\nLike, I’ll say I’m going to handle a bill or some commitment, and then it just… doesn’t get finished reliably. My husband has started taking over some of those things because he can’t really count on me to follow through, which is hard to admit.\n\nAnd at work, it’s gotten serious enough that mistakes and unfinished follow-up affected a client review, so now I’m worried people are wondering if I can actually handle the promotion. So, yes, even when I try to tell myself it’s just stress, it’s affecting more than just work.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in quiet or low-stress situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. That’s part of what makes it hard to explain, because it’s not only when things are hectic.\n\nLike, if I’m reading at home—something I want to do, not work—I’ll still end up checking something on my phone, or getting up, or humming without really noticing. Or before salsa class starts, when everyone’s just kind of waiting quietly, I get this antsy feeling and I’ll start talking or looking for something to do.\n\nIt’s like pure quiet is uncomfortable somehow. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was a kid, honestly. I don’t think it just started in adulthood.\n\nMy mom actually still jokes about this—probably not in a mean way, but you know, family jokes stick with you—that I could ace a test and then forget the project that was literally sitting in my backpack. And my elementary school report cards were kind of the same theme: “bright,” but lost assignments, rushed through little details, talked out of turn, needed reminders to finish my work.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to think that was just me being scattered or maybe bored, because I did well enough on tests. But looking back, yes, those attention and impulsive things were already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety in situations where you're expected to be quiet, like in a meeting or at home watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll try to sit still and look normal, but after a while I feel like I need to stand up or move somehow. Sometimes I’ll invent a reason to walk to the printer or get water or check something, even if it’s not really necessary.\n\nAt home it can happen too, like with a movie, though meetings are worse because I’m aware I’m supposed to be composed. I mean, unfortunately yes.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten past the “oh, she’s just busy” kind of thing. I’ve made mistakes, and the bigger issue is probably the follow-up—like I’ll mean to circle back on something, and then it’s just… not done. One client review was affected by that, which was really embarrassing. And I’ve been up for more responsibility, or at least that’s been discussed, but now I worry people are wondering if I can actually handle it.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband is pretty patient, but he’s taken over bills and things I said I would handle. Commitments I agreed to, appointments, that kind of thing. Not because he wants to control it, but because he doesn’t really trust that I’ll finish it reliably. Which is hard to say out loud, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience restlessness or fidgetiness when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the way it showed up then was maybe less like me saying “I feel restless” and more that I just couldn’t quite stay on track in the classroom.\n\nMy report cards in elementary school were always this weird mix of “bright” and then all these comments about losing assignments, rushing past the small details, talking out of turn, needing reminders to finish my work. My mom still brings up that I could do really well on a test and then completely forget the project that was sitting in my backpack the whole time.\n\nSo… yes, I think there was that kind of fidgety, unsettled thing there, but it got written up more as me being chatty or careless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention and impulsivity interfering with your daily routines or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, work is the obvious place where it shows up, but outside of work it’s definitely affecting normal day-to-day life too.\n\nAt home, my husband has kind of… quietly taken over certain things, like bills and commitments I said I’d handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. Which sounds terrible when I say it that way. It’s not that I don’t care—it’s more like I’ll agree to something, and then it slips, or I don’t follow through the way I meant to.\n\nAnd then work bleeds into it too, because when I make mistakes or don’t follow up, I carry that stress home. There was a client review that was affected, and now I worry people are questioning whether I can handle a promotion. So even when I’m home, I’m thinking about what I forgot, or what I need to fix, and then the home things pile up too. It’s all sort of tangled together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention and impulsivity significantly interfering with your ability to complete daily tasks and maintain a regular routine?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I wish it was just a matter of being a little disorganized, but it’s more than that.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow up on have gotten noticeable. There was a client review that was actually affected because I didn’t finish or circle back the way I should have, and now I keep thinking, okay, if they were considering me for a promotion, are they now wondering if I can really handle it?\n\nAnd then at home it’s the same kind of thing, just with different consequences. My husband has had to take over bills and commitments I’d already agreed to handle because, honestly, he can’t really count on me to complete them reliably. Which is embarrassing. Like, I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then somehow it’s not done, and then we’re dealing with the fallout. So yes, it does interfere with keeping a normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t, like… I don’t have a neat history to give you where I can say, “Yes, at this age, this doctor said this, and I took this medication.” It’s more that people have commented on different things over the years, or I’ve wondered about it myself, especially when stuff starts piling up.\n\nSorry, I’m probably making that less direct than you asked. And then I’m sort of scrambling and thinking, how did I not finish that?\n\nSo… I don’t know if that answers it exactly. I mean, I do fidget, but that’s not what’s getting me in trouble.\n\nThe attention and follow-through stuff is affecting real things now. At work, my mistakes and unfinished follow-up have gotten to the point where a client review was affected, and I keep worrying people are looking at me like, can she actually handle this promotion? Which is embarrassing, because I’ve worked really hard to get here.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I said I would handle because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. Not because I don’t care, but because I’ll agree to something and then it just doesn’t get completed.
